# Two-hour strategy push (25 Sept 2026, from 21:38 UTC)

Rule: data and symbols from anyone, no one's interpretations; every strategy gets a control; slim chances allowed.

Conventions: **W###** = Wells/Yajnadevam sign codes (merged corpus, `data/derived/merged-corpus-reading-order.json`), **M###** = Mahadevan IM77 numbers (`data/im77/`). Texts are in reading order, first-read-first. "Frame" = opener (W817/861/820 = M267/M391 + marker W2 = M99) … closer (X + jar W740 = M342, or arrow W520 = M211, tree W390 = M162/169, …) … suffix (W400 = M176 or person W90 = M1). Rows marked b/c are controls or corrections of the row with the same number.

| # | strategy | result | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Kober triplets / Ventris grid: stems recurring with alternating final signs | stems with alternating endings 191 vs 275 shuffled; triplets 57 vs 104. Alternating finals form one closed set of ~10 terminal signs (740, 400, 090, 151, 520, 565, 679, 621, 154, 156) plus allograph pairs 390/405/407, 220/226 | No inflection grid. One ending slot with about 10 values; no sign of a consonant × vowel structure (`strat_kober.py`) |
| 2 | Logogram vs spelling (cuneiform method): single sign X ↔ two-sign YZ in otherwise identical texts | 191 recurring X↔YZ pairs vs 0.7 shuffled, but almost all are slot alternations (e.g. 590-390 alternates with 6 different singles). Only 3 one-to-one pairs, 2 frames each: **226 ↔ 220-520**, 605 ↔ 233-001, 048 ↔ 233-803 | No spelling system found. Weak candidate: stroked fish 226 = fish 220 + 520, a modifier acting as a fused following sign (`strat_spelling.py`) |
| 3 | Modified sign = base + fused suffix? Contexts of M against contexts of (B, X) | **226 ≈ 220 + 520**: fusion distance 0.54 vs random pairs 1.34 ± 0.25 (0/40 random as good). 405/407 ≈ 390 + 151 (3% of random as good; n = 6). 741, 233, 235, 240: no. **Regional split:** fused 226 is 24/41 Mohenjo-daro, 5/41 Harappa; spelled 220-520 is 32 Mohenjo-daro / 29 Harappa (Fisher below) | **Moderate:** at least one stroke-modified sign is a fused writing of base + following sign, with a regional scribal preference (Mohenjo-daro fuses, Harappa writes out). About the writing system, not sound (`strat_fused.py`) |

The most common value (3) does not exist in a binary series. Tree counts 3–8 (peak 4) are not binary either | **No:** the leaf unit is not a weight denomination; the tablets count items, not weights |

The best Sumerian key on Indus is degenerate (sag ×13, nin ×10 …; top held-out hits sagsag 384, sagnin 154, susag 145, like Tamil's *tantan*) | **Akkadian: no signal** (Indus is no higher than the cross baseline). **Sumerian: Indus 2.3 SD above one cross baseline**; S8b settles it: Indus over 3 splits +8.02, +6.48, +6.71 (mean 7.1); cross baselines with planted Tamil, Sanskrit, English and Akkadian +5.75, +4.22, **+11.50**, +4.04 (mean 6.4). **No Sumerian signal**: Indus sits inside the range the Sumerian word list reaches on corpora of other languages. |
| 9 | Within-text sign repetition vs chance (from each script's own sign frequencies) | Observed/chance at length 4, 5, 6: **Indus 0.20, 0.17, 0.22**; Sumerian/Akkadian name spellings 1.70, 1.36, 0.75; Linear Elamite running text 0.67, 0.75, 0.77 | **Qualified by S16 (it depends on the transcription, and Ur III seal legends come close to it).** Original reading: Indus texts avoid repeating signs about 5× more than readable writing does. They are **not sound-spellings**; they behave like strings of distinct word-signs in slots (or a non-linguistic label system). This contradicts every syllabic-key approach, including this project's own key search. **Follow-up:** against a slot-preserving null (signs shuffled across texts at the same position) the ratio is still 0.24, 0.22, 0.26 (P < 0.002): the avoidance is not a side-effect of the slot grammar |

| 15 | Do seal texts depend on the animal engraved on the seal? The text does not depend on the animal: no sign names it, and the animal and the text carry independent information (the animal perhaps a group or office, the text perhaps a person). |
| 16 | Calibrate S9 (repetition avoidance) against other early systems: Proto-Elamite account entries, proto-cuneiform lines, and Ur III and Old Babylonian seal legends (CDLI) | `strat_repcal.py`. **First, S9 depends on the transcription.** On the IM77 transcription Indus shows much less avoidance (observed/slot-null 0.5–0.9), because 73 of its 99 repeats are *adjacent doubles* (e.g. M245 M245), which the Wells/Yajnadevam coding writes as one compound sign. With adjacent doubles collapsed in every corpus, the ratios (observed/slot-preserving null, lengths 4/5/6) are: **Indus IM77 0.11/0.17/0.16; Indus Yajnadevam 0.11/0.20/0.20**; Ur III seal legends 0.20/0.31/0.21; proto-cuneiform 0.16/0.31/0.44; Proto-Elamite 0.43/0.72/0.58; Old Babylonian seal legends 1.33/1.09/1.03 | **S9 corrected.** Indus avoids non-adjacent repeats more than any comparison system, but not by a different order of magnitude. Ur III seal legends (formula + name + title, with syllabic name spellings) come close (0.2–0.3). So the S9 statement "not sound-spellings" is **too strong**. The right claim: Indus behaves like a short formulaic legend or label system (Ur III seal legends, proto-cuneiform), not like running text or Old Babylonian seal prayers. The two Indus transcriptions agree once doubles are handled the same way. |
| 17 | Are seal texts unique like personal names? Which duplicates travel between cities? | IM77 complete seals (1,434 undamaged). 1,653 of 1,814 seal texts are unique; duplicated texts are short (mean 3.1 signs vs 5.1 overall). Texts of 6 or more signs **never** recur across sites (0 of 5 duplicate groups). Duplicate groups that come from a single site, observed vs expected if the site were random (MD 0.70, HP 0.17, …): 1–2 signs 9 vs 15.5 (they are *spread* across cities); 3–4 signs 19 vs 16.2; 5+ signs 9 vs 5.7. Short vs long: Fisher p = 0.004 | **Name-like pattern.** Long seal texts behave like individual names: unique, and when repeated, repeated in the same city (one owner, two seals). Short texts (1–2 signs, e.g. 267-99, 102-15, 292-342) recur in several cities more than chance, like titles, offices or institutions. This matches the Ur III seal model (name + title), with the title part shared. |
| 18 | Where do the short city-shared seal texts (S17) sit inside long seal texts? | 27 two-sign seal texts found at more than one site, located inside 1,000+ single-line seal texts of 4 or more signs. Positions (reading order) of these pairs: start 146, middle 32, end 90, against all bigrams start 881, middle 2,486, end 881 | **The shared short texts are the frame of the long ones.** 26 of the 27 are "X + closing sign" (closing signs 342, 162, 169, 15, 254, 12). Each recurs at the **end** of long texts: 296-342 ×13, 149-342 ×13, 104-169 ×11, 216-254 ×11, 124-342 ×8, 102-162 ×7. The one opener, 267-99, starts 143 long texts. So a seal text = [optional opener 267-99] + unique middle + closing pair, and a short seal carries the frame without the middle. The **unique middle is the best candidate for personal names**; the closing pairs are candidates for titles or offices. Data-only structure; no sound values. |

Top-10 *final* signs: Ur III 0.30, Indus 0.41 | **Weak, negative for the Sumerian naming model.** Indus middles have no small set of productive name-heads at their start. The concentration, modest, is at their *end* (M211, M1, M249, M99, M176). If these are names, they are built head-final (qualifier before head), unlike Sumerian names. This is a structural observation only; no language is assumed. |

Abroad it opens it, which is the order of Sumerian *lu₂ X* / Akkadian *awīl X* "man of X". With S19 (head-final middles at home), a hypothesis: foreign-found texts were composed in a head-initial West Asian word order with Indus signs. No city has its own signs (a place-name sign would show up here), and repetition is rare everywhere. |
| 106 | What *do* round seals found at home carry, if not the frame? (16 from known Indus sites) | Over-represented vs home square seals: **female-type figure W140 4 of 16 vs 59 of 1,543 (p = 0.003)**; **W592 2 of 16 vs 4 of 1,543 (p = 0.001)**, in the sequence "140-592" at Mohenjo-daro twice; stroke numbers 1 and 3 and the suffix W400 mildly (p = 0.02–0.06) | **Home round seals have their own content**: W140 (the figure that opens texts, S77) and the rare W592, not the owner frame. Small n. The round format at home seems tied to a different role (the W140 category?) rather than to foreigners, which differs from the round seals found abroad (persons + numbers, S98b). |
| 108 | Coverage: how many texts show at least one frame element (opener W817/861/820, or closer jar/arrow/tree at the end or before a suffix)? | Seals 1,117 of 1,898 (**59%**); tablets 397 of 767 (52%); tags 72 of 133 (54%); pottery 81 of 250 (32%) | **The frame is the majority pattern on seals, tablets and tags**, less common on pottery. The remaining 41% of seal texts are short labels or middles without a marked frame (cf. S17, S102). |
| 109 | Voucher values by Harappa mound, hand-incised tablets only (no mould effect) | Tall-stroke + leaf values: **Mound F 2 ×18, 3 ×43, 4 ×24; Mound E 2 ×11, 3 ×7, 4 ×36** (F vs E χ² p < 1e-5) | **Different standard values in different parts of the city**: 3 dominates on Mound F, 4 on Mound E, on hand-made tablets. Either two issuing institutions with different standard units, or the time shift of S95 (the mounds' excavated deposits differ in phase). S95's mould objection does not apply here. A real, spatially structured administrative standard. |
| 109b | Control for S109: separate place from time | Same phase (3B/C) only: Mound E 2 ×6, 3 ×6, 4 ×7; Mound F 2 ×10, 3 ×34, 4 ×20. Mound E's strong 4-dominance comes largely from **19 unphased tablets all with value 4** (probably one context or cache) | **Weakened again.** Within one phase the mounds differ only mildly (value 3 is 53% on F vs 32% on E; χ² p = 0.17). The big E vs F contrast rests on a single group of nineteen 4-leaf tablets. A deposit-level effect (one issue or batch), not yet a citywide standard difference. |
| 82b | Relation to the existing identity directory | `data/derived/identity-directory.json` (earlier work) already lists 386 recurring core identities, 36 of them with sealing sites ("shipments"), keyed on frame-stripped cores | **S81–S83 overlap with that directory.** The Lothal-tag / Mohenjo-daro-seal match (817-2-48-740) is a full-text match; the directory works on cores. The two approaches agree that texts travel as sealed goods between cities. |
| 110 | What are the single signs written on a separate second line (S27: M374, M242, M93, M393)? | Their positions whenever they appear: M374 **only ever alone** (9 of 9); M393 alone 5 of 6; M242 final 13, alone 9; M93 final 4, alone 5 | **Stand-alone or closing signs.** Signs set on their own line never sit inside a text: they are free-standing labels or closers, written apart like a title or emblem-name. That fits the frame, where closers are separable units (S17, S18). |
| 111 | Are vouchers issued under titles or under names? Text faces of Harappa count + text tablets (277) | Title-only faces (at most one sign + jar/suffix, e.g. 176-740-400): 67 (24%); other short faces (≤2 signs): 38; longer faces with a core: 172 (62%) | **Mostly under names or cores, a quarter under a bare title.** Most vouchers carry a core (issuer or recipient identity); about a quarter carry only a title such as 176-740(-400), the formula that takes several leaf values (S93, S96). An institution issuing standard amounts under its title, beside many individually named vouchers. |
| 112 | Are title-only seals (≤3 signs ending in jar/arrow/comb/person) concentrated in one Mohenjo-daro district, as office seals of one institution might be? | DK 14% (140 of 1,003), HR 18% (30 of 170), VS 9% (6 of 70); χ² p = 0.17 | **Null.** Title-only seals are spread across the city's districts; no district looks like an administrative centre. |
| 107 | **Decisive positive control: can the key search read a real seal corpus in a known language?** Ur III seal legends (3,200 CDLI legends, readings replaced by opaque codes), searched with the Sumerian lexicon (`strat_ur3_control.py`) | Gap over fake lexicons **+4.71 SD (PASS)**, fixed-key z = 12.0. But the best key gets **0 of 80 signs right**: it maps a dozen different signs to *gal*, others to *lu*, *la*, *gin*, *sag* (a degenerate key like the Indus ones, S8). The true readings (a, dingir, kur, sipa, ...) are not recovered | **The key-search method cannot decipher even a known language in a seal corpus of this size and genre.** It "passes" while being entirely wrong. Consequences: (1) every key "pass" in this project, Indus or control, is not a decipherment; (2) the Indus nulls say nothing about which language is behind the script; (3) statistical key-fitting on short formulaic texts is a dead end, and only an outside anchor (a bilingual, or names known from elsewhere) can give readings. The Akkadian cross run is in RESULTS.md. |
| 113 | **Audit of every "power" control: do passing keys recover the planted readings?** Best real key vs the true secret mapping, top-80 signs | Planted Tamil (noise 0): gap **+6.39 SD PASS, 2 of 80 signs correct**; planted Tamil (noise 0.2): +5.31 PASS, 1 of 80; planted Sanskrit: 0 of 80; planted Sumerian (S8): +5.79 PASS, **0 of 80**; Tamil on middles (S31): +5.36 PASS, 3 of 80; real Ur III legends (S107): +4.71 PASS, 0 of 80. Passing keys are degenerate (many signs → sag / gal / tan / ku) | **Major correction to the whole key-search programme.** The "power" controls never showed that the search can *recover* a language. They showed only that a degenerate key hits more lexicon words on a language-derived corpus than on fakes. The gap statistic measures lexicon-hit inflation, not decipherment. So (a) "power OK" throughout RESULTS.md means "a signal of language-like text", not "able to decode"; (b) no calibrated search in this project could ever have produced a dictionary; (c) the Indus nulls are uninformative about language identity. A future search needs a validator scored on key *accuracy* in planted controls. |
| 114 | Can the objective be fixed by forcing an **injective** key (each sound used by one sign only, like the true planted key)? (`strat_injective.py`, planted Tamil, noise 0, 4 runs × 60,000 iterations) | Found keys score **2,339–2,483 lexicon hits vs 1,313 for the true key**; correct signs 0, 0, 0, 1 of 80 | **No. The objective is non-identifiable at this corpus size.** Even among one-to-one keys, many wrong keys spell more "Tamil words" than the true key does, because a large lexicon (tens of thousands of forms) is matched by chance by short windows. Better optimisation cannot help; the truth is not the optimum. **Answer to "will you ever get a dictionary this way": no.** Statistical key-fitting on 2–5-sign windows of about 4,000 short texts cannot identify a language or its sound values, even when the language is known and planted. Readings can only come from outside anchors: bilingual names, loanwords with known referents, or much longer texts. |
| 115 | Would more text make the key identifiable? Planted Tamil, injective search: real text shapes vs 5× as many texts vs texts 3× longer (`strat_scale.py`) | True key vs found key (lexicon-window hits): base 1,313 vs 2,338 (1 of 80 correct); **5× texts 6,634 vs 11,575 (2 correct)**; **3× longer 5,813 vs 8,824 (1 correct)** | **More data does not fix it.** The window-hit objective itself is wrong: wrong keys beat the true key by 50–75% at every scale. Short lexicon forms are matched everywhere by chance. A usable objective must explain *whole texts* (segmentation coverage or likelihood), not count hits. Tested next (S116). |
| 116 | A whole-text objective: share of sign tokens covered by a segmentation into lexicon words (3+ letters), planted Tamil | **True key covers 67%** of tokens; **the degenerate found key covers 96%** | **Coverage fails too.** A key that maps many signs to a few very productive syllables (*tan*, *ku*, …) parses almost anything into "words". Any lexicon-matching objective without a strong penalty on sound reuse is fooled. Also, the planted corpus itself is only 67% parseable by its true key, because texts start and end mid-word. Tamil (like Sumerian, Sanskrit) has so many short forms that short texts are always "readable" under some wrong key. **This is the core reason the Indus script cannot be read by statistics alone.** |
| 117 | Both safeguards together: injective key **and** whole-text coverage objective (`strat_injcov.py`, planted Tamil) | Two runs: found keys parse **100% of sign tokens into Tamil words, with 0 of 80 signs correct**; the true key parses 67% | **Definitive negative.** With a real Tamil lexicon, a wrong one-to-one sign-to-syllable key can make *every* short text read as Tamil, better than the true key does. This is why rival "decipherments" (Dravidian, Sanskrit, Sumerian …) can each look convincing: short formulaic texts plus a large lexicon allow a perfect-looking wrong reading. Any claimed Indus reading that rests on words "found" in the texts is worthless unless it also predicts something outside the corpus. |
| 118 | **How many outside anchors would be needed?** Planted Tamil, injective key; the k most frequent signs fixed to their true values; the rest searched (`strat_anchors_needed.py`) | Correct among the free signs: k = 0 → 1 of 80; **k = 10 → 0 of 70; k = 20 → 1 of 60; k = 40 → 6 of 40**. The found keys still out-score the true key at every k (1,546–2,338 vs 1,313 hits) | **Even knowing half the signs (the 40 commonest) recovers only 15% of the rest by statistics.** In practice, readings must come sign by sign from outside anchors (bilingual names, loanwords, iconic signs with known referents), each checked by predictions, not from fitting lexicons to texts. This bounds what any computational approach, including this project's, can deliver from the present corpus. |
| 119 | Best case for statistics: **15× the tokens** (texts 3× longer and 5× more numerous) **plus anchors** (planted Tamil, injective key; `strat_best_case.py`) | 180,534 windows. Correct among free signs: k = 0 → 0 of 80; k = 20 → 1 of 60; k = 40 → 2 of 40. Found keys still beat the true key (32,129–39,294 vs 29,507 hits) | **Even a corpus 15 times larger, with half the signs anchored, does not let lexicon-fitting recover the rest.** The failure is in the method class (matching a large lexicon), not in the amount of Indus text. Any future reading needs a different kind of evidence per sign. |
| 120 | The classical cipher-breaking method: an injective key scored by a **syllable-bigram language model** of Tamil (built from the lexicon), not by word hits (`strat_lm.py`, planted Tamil) | Three runs: found keys score log-likelihood −8,427 to −8,744 vs **−9,262 for the true key**; correct signs 1, 0, 5 of 80 (2,909 tokens in segments fully covered by the top 80 signs) | **Fails as well.** A syllable language model is also better satisfied by a wrong key than by the truth when the text is this short and fragmented. Cipher-style decoding works on long continuous texts; the Indus corpus is thousands of 4–5-sign fragments. With S113–S119, every family of statistical objective tried (word hits, coverage, n-gram likelihood; free or injective; with more text; with anchors) fails on a *known, planted* language. |
| 121 | Sanity check for S120: the same LM decoder on **long continuous** planted Tamil (75 texts × 200 signs) | Only 6,201 of 15,000 tokens fall in runs of the top-80 signs; found keys −23,238 / −24,750 / −23,262 vs true −24,224; correct 0, 0, 4 of 80 | **Also fails, and this sharpens the diagnosis.** The planted Tamil "script" has hundreds of syllable signs (like Indus's ~400). Decoding only the 80 commonest leaves the text fragmented even when the texts are long, and fragments are cheap to fit wrongly. A large sign inventory is itself a barrier: grid and cipher methods have worked on small syllabaries (Linear B has about 90 signs), not on large logo-syllabic inventories. A logo-syllabic script with 400+ signs is not solvable this way at any realistic corpus size. |
| 107b | S107 cross baseline: the same coded Ur III legends searched with **Akkadian** | Akkadian +1.88 SD (fail) vs Sumerian +4.71 (pass), both with wrong keys | **Nuance: the gap statistic can rank the right language above a wrong one on real data** (Sumerian over Akkadian here), without producing correct readings. But S8b showed the Sumerian lexicon reaching +11.5 on planted English. So the ranking is unreliable across lexicons of different sizes and shapes. For Indus, no language stood out from its own cross-baseline range (S8, S8b, S31). |
| 122 | Easiest possible planted case: every text is whole Tamil words (no mid-word starts or cut-offs), Indus text counts and lengths (`strat_easy.py`) | Found injective keys 3,479–3,777 hits vs **true key 2,736**; correct 2, 1, 0 of 80 | **Still fails.** Even when texts are clean sequences of whole words, wrong one-to-one keys make more lexicon words than the truth. The non-identifiability is intrinsic to matching a large lexicon against short texts written in a large syllabary. It is not an artefact of how the controls were built. |
| 123 | Is lexicon size the problem? Same clean texts as S122, but the objective uses **only the 3,000 words actually used to write them** (the "right small vocabulary") (`strat_smalllex.py`) | Found keys 1,910–2,050 hits vs **true 1,770**; correct 0, 0, 0 of 80 | **No: even knowing the exact vocabulary, wrong keys win.** With hundreds of syllable signs and texts of 4–5 signs, too many alternative assignments spell real words. **The Indus corpus, as short texts in a large sign inventory, is not decipherable by any internal statistical method tested here, whatever the language.** Only external, sign-specific anchors can work (ANCHORS §19). |
| 124 | **Contrast: a small syllabary.** Planted Tamil written only with its 80 commonest syllables (every token decoded), clean whole-word texts, Indus text counts and lengths, right vocabulary (`strat_smallsyll.py`) | **True key 3,091 hits > found keys 2,849–2,940**; correct 0–1 of 80 | **The objective becomes identifiable** (the truth is now the optimum), and the remaining failure is only a search problem that better optimisation could solve. So the barrier for Indus is specific: a **large sign inventory with short texts** (S121–S123), not statistics as such. A script with about 80 phonetic signs and this much text would be crackable; Indus, with about 400 signs used logo-syllabically in 4–5-sign formulae, is not. |
| 125 | Where does identifiability break? Same design as S124 with a syllabary of 150 and 300 signs (top 80 decoded, as for Indus) (`strat_syllsize.py`) | **80 signs: true 3,091 > found ~2,900 (identifiable)**; **150 signs: found 2,462 > true 2,201**; **300 signs: found 2,102 > true 1,992**; correct 0 in both | **The threshold is somewhere between 80 and 150 signs.** Once the inventory is well above the number of signs that can be decoded together, wrong keys win. The Indus inventory (about 400 signs, a long tail of rare ones) sits far beyond that threshold, as does Proto-Elamite (about 1,000 signs). Linear A, by contrast, has a small syllabary, so its obstacle is the unknown language, not this one. |

| 126 | **Revises S125:** decode *all* signs instead of the top 80 (150- and 300-sign syllabaries, same design; `strat_syllsize_n.py`) | 150 signs, all decoded: **true 2,674 > found 2,552**; 300 signs, all decoded: **true 2,934 > found 1,997** (4 correct) | **With the whole inventory decoded, the truth is the optimum again**, even at 300 signs. The loss of identifiability in S121–S125 comes from **decoding only a subset** (the rare signs break texts into fragments that wrong keys fit easily), not from inventory size itself. What remains is a hard *search* problem: at 300 signs the annealer ends far below the truth. For Indus this means: (a) the obstacle is the long tail of rare signs, which cannot be keyed from a few occurrences; (b) a search over the full inventory with the *right* vocabulary might in principle converge, but the language, the vocabulary and whether signs are syllables at all are unknown, and S117 shows that a large wrong lexicon still gives perfect-looking fits. Net answer unchanged: no reading without outside anchors. |
| 127 | **Revises S126:** harder search (200,000 iterations), 150-sign syllabary, all signs decoded (`strat_fullinv.py`) | Right 3,000-word vocabulary: **found 2,692 > true 2,674**, 1 correct. Full 62,868-word Tamil lexicon: **found 4,418 > true 3,814**, 0 correct | **S126's recovery was an artefact of weak search.** With a longer search, wrong keys beat the truth even when every sign is decoded and the exact vocabulary is known, and by far more with a realistic large lexicon. Final answer: for a script like Indus (hundreds of signs, 4–5-sign texts), matching sounds to a language by statistics does not work. Readings need outside anchors. |
| 128 | How big is the Indus rare-sign tail that S121–S127 identify as the barrier? (merged Wells corpus, all objects) | 713 sign types (Wells codes, including variants) in 17,829 tokens; **219 hapax, 383 signs seen 3 times or fewer**; the top 80 signs cover 80% of tokens (top 150: 89%); median text length **3 signs**; only 53% of texts are written entirely in the top 80 signs | **Indus is worse than every planted control.** Its texts are shorter (median 3 vs about 4–5 in the controls), and half of them contain a sign outside the 80 commonest. Hundreds of signs occur too rarely to be assigned any value statistically. The conditions under which the controls already failed (S113–S127) are exceeded on every axis. |
| 129 | **The constructive case:** 150-sign planted syllabary, all signs decoded, right vocabulary, **with the 40 (or 75) commonest signs anchored** to their true values (`strat_anchor150.py`) | **40 anchors → 95 of the other 110 signs recovered (86%)**; 75 anchors → 72 of 75 (96%); found scores 2,661 / 2,670 vs true 2,674 | **This is what decipherment would need.** Once about a quarter of the sign inventory is pinned by outside evidence *and* the right vocabulary is known, the search recovers most of the rest. Compare S118: with a large lexicon and only the top 80 signs decoded, 40 anchors gave 6 of 40. So the realistic route for Indus is (1) outside anchors for a few dozen common signs, (2) a restricted domain vocabulary (names, titles, commodities) in the right language, (3) a key over the whole inventory. Steps 1 and 2 need evidence that does not exist yet (ANCHORS §19). |
| 130 | How few anchors suffice, and does it survive a big generic lexicon? (150-sign planted syllabary, all decoded; `strat_anchor_min.py`) | Right vocabulary: **10 anchors → 5 of 140 recovered; 20 anchors → 26 of 130 (20%)**; 40 anchors → 95 of 110 (S129). Full 62,868-word Tamil lexicon with 40 anchors → **62 of 110 (56%)** | **A threshold between about 20 and 40 anchors** (15–25% of the inventory). Above it, the rest largely falls into place, even with a generic large dictionary (56%). Below it, almost nothing is recovered. For a 400-sign script, this means on the order of 60–100 securely anchored signs. |
| 131 | **Direct test of the only published outside-anchor proposal**: Fuls 2024's 20 Indus signs with Linear Elamite values, read against Elamite word lists: 304 Old Elamite forms from the HLED Linear Elamite corpus and 3,004 later Elamite forms from CDLI (`strat_fuls_anchor.py`) | Indus windows made only of anchored signs that read as an Elamite word: **2 (azu, hise)** vs 1.7 with the 20 values shuffled among the signs (P = 0.43) and 1.2 with random LE syllables (P = 0.29) | **No support.** Read with these values, the Indus texts produce no more Elamite words than chance assignments. This agrees with the earlier blind test of the same key (ANCHORS: p = 0.83). S130 predicts that 20 anchors would be too few to complete a key even if they were right; here they do not even pass the direct check. |
| 132 | **Power of the S131 anchor test**, plus a reusable tool (`anchor_test.py`): the same test with 20 *true* anchors on planted corpora with Indus text shapes (noise 0.2) | Planted Elamite (3,004-form list): **observed 648 vs 191 shuffled (P = 0.01), vs 124 random (P = 0.004): detected**. Planted Tamil (62,868 forms): 845 vs 656 (P = 0.17): **not detected** | **The anchor test has power when the word list is small** (Elamite-sized) and none when it is huge (Tamil-sized: too many chance words). So S131's null against Elamite word lists is informative: if Fuls's 20 values were right *and* the texts were Elamite, the test would most likely have shown it. The tool `anchor_test.py anchors.json wordlist.txt` can now test any future anchor proposal, with `calibrate` for its power. |
| 133 | **Anchor threshold at Indus scale**: planted Tamil with a ~400-sign syllabary (304 signs in the training half, all decoded), right vocabulary, k commonest signs anchored (`strat_anchor400.py`) | Recovered among free signs: **k = 0 → 3 of 304 (1%); k = 20 → 38 of 284 (13%); k = 40 → 116 of 264 (44%); k = 60 → 146 of 244 (60%); k = 100 → 142 of 204 (70%)**. The true key scores highest in every case (2,884 vs 2,346–2,843) | **The anchor route scales.** With about 40–60 anchored common signs (13–20% of the inventory) and the right vocabulary, roughly half of the rest becomes recoverable, even for a 300–400-sign script. The rare-sign tail stays out of reach (70% at most). For Indus the requirement is therefore about **40–60 secure anchors plus the right language's vocabulary**, somewhat fewer than the 60–100 estimated from S130. |
| 134 | Realistic anchors at Indus scale (~400-sign planted syllabary, 60 anchors; `strat_anchor_noisy.py`) | (a) 60 correct anchors, **generic big lexicon** (all Tamil forms): 63 of 244 recovered (26%, vs 60% with the right vocabulary, S133); found 4,179 slightly above true 4,157. (b) **60 anchors, 12 of them wrong**, right vocabulary: 103 of 244 (42%, vs 60% with clean anchors) | **Degrades but does not collapse.** A 20% error rate in the anchors costs about a third of the recovery; not knowing the domain vocabulary costs more than half. So the most valuable outside evidence is (1) the language and a vocabulary of the right domain (names, titles, commodities), then (2) a few dozen anchors, which can tolerate some errors. |
| 135 | **A logo-syllabic planted script (closer to Indus)**: the 60 commonest Tamil words each written with one word-sign (30% of tokens), all other words spelt with 150 syllable signs; Indus text counts and lengths; right vocabulary (`strat_logosyll.py`) | Word-signs known in advance, syllables searched: k = 0 anchors → 3 of 150; **k = 20 → 130 of 130 (100%)**; k = 40 → 110 of 110. Word-signs *not* known (the search may give any sign "no sound value"): k = 0 → 29 of 210; **k = 20 → 155 of 190 (82%)**; k = 40 → 160 of 170 (94%) | **Good news for the anchor route.** When the commonest words are written with word-signs, as the Indus frame suggests (S37, S57, S88), the syllabic remainder is spelt in longer, rarer words that are much harder to fit by chance, so **about 20 anchors plus the right vocabulary suffice**. The structural work (which signs are grammatical/word-signs, GRAMMAR.md) therefore directly lowers the anchor requirement. Still required: the right language and domain vocabulary, and ~20 secure sound anchors, none of which exists today. |
| 136 | S135 with the realistic handicap: a **generic big lexicon** (all 62,868 Tamil forms) instead of the domain vocabulary; word-signs not known in advance | k = 0 → 26 of 210; **k = 20 → 58 of 190 (31%); k = 40 → 101 of 170 (59%)**. The **true key still scores highest** (2,687 vs 2,254–2,560) | **Workable but harder.** With a generic dictionary, about 40 anchors are needed for most of the rest. The truth remains the optimum, so better search (more restarts, anchor-seeded annealing) would recover more. Summary of the anchor route for a logo-syllabic script like Indus: **~20 anchors with a domain vocabulary, ~40 with a generic dictionary, in the right language.** |
| 137 | **Can anchors also identify the language?** Planted Tamil logo-syllabic script (word-signs unknown), 20 true anchors; complete the key with each candidate generic lexicon using the true anchors vs the same values shuffled among the anchor signs (2 seeds each; `strat_langid_anchor.py`) | Tamil lexicon: true anchors **2,179 / 2,132** vs shuffled 1,645 / 1,620 (**+32%**). Sanskrit lexicon: true 972 / 927 vs shuffled 886 / 841 (**+10%**) | **Yes.** With correct anchors, the right language gains much more from them than a wrong one does. So a set of about 20 secure sound anchors would do two jobs: test the anchors themselves (true vs shuffled), and pick the language by which lexicon benefits most. This turns a future anchor proposal into a falsifiable, language-discriminating test. (The size of the gain in a wrong language should be calibrated per lexicon, as S8b showed that lexicons differ in fit.) |
| 138 | Sizing the real Indus problem for the anchor route: remove the 33 frame/grammatical/numeral signs (openers, markers, closers, suffixes, strokes, fish, leaf, crescent; GRAMMAR.md) and look at what would have to be read phonetically (`strat_frame_removed.py`) | Residual: 6,635 tokens, **680 sign types, 234 hapax**; 3,931 runs with **median length 1**, only 648 runs of 3+ signs. Top 20 residual signs cover 38% of residual tokens, top 40 53%, top 80 68%, top 150 80% | **Much harder than the planted controls.** After the frame is removed, the "spelt" material is mostly isolated single signs, not multi-sign words, and the inventory is huge and long-tailed. The planted logo-syllabic control (S135) had 150 syllable signs and word-length runs. The anchor route would need both secure anchors and a much larger corpus of multi-sign names, or else the middles are themselves largely word-signs (consistent with S26, S53, S88). Then sound values may matter far less than meanings. |
| 139 | **A functional dictionary** (what each sign does, computed from data): the 120 commonest signs, with positional profile, numbers written before them, seal/tablet leaning and a rule-based class (`build_functional_dictionary.py` → `FUNCTIONAL-DICTIONARY.md`) | 60 core/name elements, 14 opening elements, 10 counted items, 10 numerals, 7 closing elements, 7 titles before the jar, 6 number-names (one fixed number), 3 openers, the jar closer, 2 connective markers | **The part of a dictionary the corpus can give.** Each sign gets a function, not a sound or a translation. This is the honest deliverable for "do we have the dictionary": functions yes, sounds and meanings no. It also constrains any future proposal: markers, openers and titles cannot be syllables of names, and only the 60 core signs are candidates for phonetic spelling. |

Boundary cases between name elements and opening elements are the least certain entries. |
| 141 | Are counted signs more often pictures of countable objects (iconic meaning anchors)? Shape descriptions classed as object (fish, tree, jar, arrow, leaf, person, …) vs geometric | Counted items and number-names: 6 object vs 3 geometric; core/name elements 15 vs 15; frame signs 10 vs 6 (Fisher counted vs core p = 0.46) | **Null.** Counted signs are not detectably more pictorial than name elements. | 142 | Which signs are most worth an outside anchor? Rank function-compatible signs (core, counted, number-name, opening) by frequency, number of sites and presence in foreign/round-seal texts | Top of the list: fish W220, tree W390, twelve-stroke grid W55, leaf W700, female figure W140, pitchfork W415, tree W405, W255, W590, W384 (25 listed in ANCHORS §20) | **A concrete target list** for anyone looking for outside evidence (bilingual names, loanwords, iconic referents). Following S133–S136, 20–40 secured signs of this kind, plus the language's vocabulary, is the threshold for recovering the rest. |
| 143 | Better search for the realistic anchor case (logo-syllabic planted script, word-signs unknown, 20 anchors, generic big lexicon): 4 restarts × 150,000 iterations (`strat_better_search.py`) | Best restart 2,254 hits, 58 of 190 correct; others 45–56. A smarter optimiser is the open technical problem. |
| 144 | A different search: anchor-seeded greedy growth (assign signs one at a time, most frequent first, scoring only windows whose signs are all assigned; `strat_greedy.py`) | Right vocabulary: 20 anchors → 26 of 190; 40 → 56 of 170. Generic lexicon: 20 → 18 of 190; 40 → 32 of 170 | **Worse than annealing** (S135/S136: 155 and 58 of 190 with 20 anchors). Early greedy mistakes propagate. The arrow complex itself is missed because its A is a class (the fish words) and its X a pair (705/706-33). | **A second phrase template, weak on its own but matching S298's independent observation (176-100 ×13, never 100-176): SEATED-PERSON (+ THREE-HEADED PERSON) + JAR.** The three-headed figure is an optional middle member of the 'seated person … jar' title. The search count itself (1) is at the edge of the null (max 1), so this stays a lead. Class-level rerun (fish family and 705/706 collapsed; `strat_optinsert_cls.py`) finds the same single pattern, with null max 1. The arrow complex is still missed because its X is the common tall 3, which also occurs elsewhere in fish texts. So the search is conservative, and the arrow complex rests on the targeted test S300. |

| S303 | Do closing words choose their own qualifiers? W156/M15: 3 or M389. | **Real, and replicated: closers select their own qualifier classes, like head nouns taking their own modifiers or classifiers.** This turns the closing slot into about ten small title phrases, each with a known head and a known set of qualifiers. The next sign token is a sign we have never seen about 1.7% of the time, and the full inventory is about 950 signs (Chao1), so about 240 remain unseen.** The next sign pair is new 28% of the time, and **the next text of 3+ signs is new 95% of the time.** Mohenjo-daro and Harappa supply 76% of all texts. | **The frame and phrase results are safe, the names are not.** Common signs and their positions are well sampled: frequent patterns (openers, closers, fish order, phrase templates) rest on hundreds of cases and replicate in two corpora, so a small sample does not bias those rates. The name slot is almost all unseen: nearly every new seal will carry a name we have never seen. Claims of 'never' (e.g. 0 of 86) are upper bounds, not proofs: by the rule of three, 0 of n means under 3/n. Many results are really Mohenjo-daro + Harappa results; each should be rechecked on the other sites when possible. |
| S310 | **Fresh start: parse every text with all grammar found so far** (opener + marker; count = numeral + item; title phrase = the closer's own qualifiers learned on a random half of the texts, plus the fish stack, 705/706-33 and W176/W100; closer; suffix). Commonest shapes: NAME alone (727), NAME + TITLE + CLOSER (447), NAME + CLOSER (361), NAME + COUNT (339). (b) The name reading of the middle does not follow from uniqueness. The S311 name-internal structure stands as structure, but 'name' is a gloss, not a result. The largest group: **10 Lothal sealings (TAG:B, SRG area) carry 817-2-48-740, a text known from one Mohenjo-daro seal.** Others: Lothal sealings with Mohenjo-daro seal texts 705-33-520, 140-100-740 and 861-2-235; a Kalibangan sealing (861-2-3-390), a Hulas sealing (590-407-740) and a Rupar sealing (17-585-740) with Mohenjo-daro seal texts; a Harappa sealing (415-220-520), also written on 15 Harappa tablets. A direct physical outside link inside the Indus world; lead for ANCHORS. |

| S326 | Five trade-logic tests (user: 'be creative'). (1) Branch map: how many emblems per repeated seal text vs emblem labels permuted (2,000×). (5) Gulf link: texts found abroad that share a whole text or a 3-sign run with home texts | **(1)** 27 repeated seal texts carry 1.30 emblems on average vs 1.48 by chance (P = 0.053). A text mostly stays with one animal, but some cross: 13-840-740 with unicorn and elephant; 3-140-100-740 unicorn at Harappa, zebu at Mohenjo-daro; 703-615-740 buffalo at Harappa, unicorn at Mohenjo-daro. **(2)** Flow runs only outward from Mohenjo-daro: → Lothal 13, Harappa 1, Kalibangan 1, Hulas 1, Rupar 1; Harappa → Lothal 1; nothing flows into Mohenjo-daro. **(3)** One text dominates (817-2-48-740, 10 sealings). **(4)** **Zebu seals are the largest: 31.0 mm vs unicorn 27.4 at Mohenjo-daro (p = 0.0004)**; elephant, gaur, rhinoceros and tiger are not different (p = 0.23–0.78); seals without an animal are smallest (21.5). **(5)** **The Kish (Mesopotamia) and Gonur Depe (Central Asia) seals both end in 590-390-740 (box-on-stand · tree · jar), a home title ending found on 30+ home seals and tablets.** The Tell Umma seal shares '2-4-390' with 5 home texts. | Four leads. (a) The text is usually tied to one house or emblem, with a minority crossing animals: branches of the same office. (b) Sealed goods flow out of Mohenjo-daro to the south-east port (Lothal) and the north, not back. (c) **The zebu is the big-seal emblem**, a candidate for the highest-status house. (d) **The same home title '590-390-740' reached Kish and Gonur Depe**: a home office or firm operating abroad, the best outside-trade link so far after the Gulf twins. |

| S347 | **User hypothesis: the whole seal is a title or credential 'like a degree': shield (opener), then what the holder commands or owns.** Predictions: (1) texts are built from standard parts: share of seal texts fully segmentable into chunks (1–3 signs) that recur in other texts, vs texts shuffled within themselves (20×). Together with S344 (the same title in several cities), S321–S322 (middles are not name stocks) and S336 (nameless quantity seals), the seal reads best as **a credential: [shield/opener] + [what the holder commands or owns: goods and counts, fish-class words] + [office word: jar, arrow …]**, not as a personal name. The size–length link is suggestive but confounded by space. |

(c) twins W91 sits beside antelope, rhino, empty field, 3 bulls, mating pair: motif-independent (p = 0.93). | S363 | **Which known NON-LINGUISTIC sign system does Indus resemble?** Library of 7 machine-readable systems: heraldry (Rietstap, 31,814 blazons), khipu (Open Khipu Repository 2.1, 6,561 cord clusters), Ur III seal legends (6,381), Proto-Elamite entries (10,836), Latin inscriptions (EDH 6,000), Linear B lines (13,170), plus an Indus-bigram self-control; 18-statistic battery, length-matched, z-scored, 200× bootstrap (`tools/strat_codelibrary.py`, `data/codelib/`, `data/derived/strat_codelibrary.txt`). Samian stamps, merchants' marks and tamgas had no machine-readable corpus | **Nearest known system: Proto-Elamite** (distance 3.2–3.4; bootstrap mean rank 1.6), then khipu 3.4, Linear B, heraldry, Ur III names, Latin (8.5); same at all merge levels and for seals alone. Drivers: within-text repetition far below shuffle (0.27 vs PE 0.23; languages 0.9–1.8), frame counts, first-position entropy ≈ middle. Heraldry fails (closed tincture opener, 10 frames cover 62% of blazons vs 15% of Indus); names fail (100% unique, open at both ends). **Residue no system reproduces:** whole texts repeat MORE than their own bigram model predicts (ratio 0.81–0.83; everything else 0.84–1.08; seals alone 0.98, so the excess comes from tablets and pots) together with an open first slot and a tiny closed end class | Indus is an accounting-type notation, closest to Proto-Elamite entries, not to heraldry or to name legends. The user's 'shield/heraldry' picture fails on structure (blazons close the START, Indus closes the END). The Indus-specific target is stock-text repetition on tablets and pots inside an end-closed frame: a stock of standard entries re-issued, i.e. tokens/vouchers of fixed categories. Caveats: OCR noise, khipu coding is a choice, 76% Mohenjo-daro + Harappa |
| S364 | **Structural alignment of three accounting-type systems** (`tools/strat_crossalign.py`; results `data/derived/strat_crossalign.txt/.json`). Language-independent behaviour vectors per frequent sign (15 features: text/line first-last, relative position, alone-in-line, numeral adjacency, item-side numeral, numeral class shares small/large/other-system, numeral variety, within-text repetition, context entropy, is-numeral). Roles labelled where known: PE M157/M327 OPENER, M297/M002/M036/M243 MEASURED, M263/M346/M264/M003 COUNTED, M387/M370/M124 QUALIFIER, medial-biased signs NAME-LIKE; LinA GRA/VIN/OLE/CYP/OLIV MEASURED, VIR/CAP COUNTED, KU-RO/KI-RO TOTAL, tablet-heading words OPENER, multi-sign list-entry words NAME-LIKE; numerals NUMERAL. Regularised multinomial logistic regression; two framings (document = tablet; document = entry/line); Indus seq_raw and seq_all. Controls: PE→LinA and LinA→PE transfer vs label-permuted training; Indus shuffled within text | **The cross-system control fails.** PE→LinA accuracy 0.25–0.32 (permuted 0.15, p 0.09–0.25), LinA→PE 0.21–0.30 (permuted 0.25, p 0.4–0.6); without the NUMERAL class both are at chance. Only the commodity split transfers, one way: PE→LinA MEASURED/COUNTED/TOTAL/QUALIFIER 5 of 7 right (p 0.08), LinA→PE 0.5 (p 0.4). The NAME-LIKE class is structurally different in the two systems (PE names sit before a class sign, LinA names take the numeral directly). Indus (93 signs ≥ 20 tokens): 18% (tablet framing) / 31% (line framing) land in a role at > 0.7 confidence, vs 6% / 18% for shuffled texts; shuffled texts keep the same role 52–59% of the time. Role assignments flip with the framing (openers 817/861/820/920/692 = OPENER 0.67–0.91 under tablet framing, QUALIFIER 0.4–0.5 under line framing; the jar W740 drifts between NUMERAL, MEASURED and COUNTED). Stable across framings: tree signs W390/W405/W407 = COMMODITY-COUNTED (0.76–0.97, the PE M263 type); closers W520, W700, W226 = MEASURED and W527, W156, W154/158 = COUNTED, but that split only re-encodes S70's tall/short stroke systems (tall was coded as the 'other' numeral system) and so is not independent evidence. No Indus sign resembles KU-RO: TOTAL probability ≤ 0.05 for every sign except W555 (0.49, one framing, n = 23) | **Dead end as a decoder, informative as a negative.** Behaviour vectors do not carry role information between Proto-Elamite and Linear A (two systems whose roles are partly known), so they cannot be trusted to read Indus roles. What survives is weak and mostly already known: the tree signs behave like PE counted commodities, the Indus closers look like PE/LinA commodity signs rather than totals or headers, and no total word exists. The credential model (WORKING-DICTIONARY §11, closers = offices) is **neither confirmed nor refuted**: the only cross-script analogue of an office/title class in the training data (PE QUALIFIER prefixes) never attracts the Indus closers, and the 'commodity' label the closers receive is driven by their numeral adjacency, which the credential model also predicts ('of 3 fish', 'of 2 jars'). Caution: the training set is 61 labelled items, PE is 95% Susa, LinA 65% Hagia Triada. |

## Summary (updated after the push: 141 strategies)

### Scoreboard: strongest results of the push

| Finding | Rows | Strength | Replicated? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Statistical key-fitting as run here cannot read a planted or real known language: passing keys are wrong, and wrong keys beat the true key whenever only the common signs are decoded | S107, S113–S123 | Decisive | 8 objective, data and vocabulary variants |
| Identifiability holds only for a small (80-sign) syllabary. At 150+ signs, wrong keys win even with every sign decoded and the exact vocabulary known | S124–S127 | Strong (contrast controls) | 3 inventory sizes, 2 search lengths |
| Constructive: 40 outside anchors (of 150 signs) + right vocabulary → 86% of the remaining signs recovered | S129 | Strong (planted) | 2 anchor levels |
| Anchor route at Indus scale: 40–60 anchors for a 400-sign syllabary; about 20 (domain vocabulary) or 40 (generic dictionary) for a logo-syllabic script; the anchors also pick the language | S133–S137 | Strong (planted) | noisy anchors and wrong lexicon tested |
| The only published anchor set (Fuls 2024, 20 Linear Elamite values) gives no more Elamite words than chance, with a test shown to have power | S131, S132 | Solid null | 2 Elamite word lists |
| Functional dictionary of the 120 commonest signs (function, not sound); classes agree across Mohenjo-daro and Harappa 78% vs 21% chance | S139, S140 | Strong | cross-city |
| No candidate language (Tamil, Sanskrit, Sumerian, Akkadian, Telugu, Malayalam, Pali, Santali) stands out | S8, S8b, S31, S55 | Solid null | 2 corpora |
| Frame grammar (opener, marker, middle, closer, suffix) with non-local constraints beyond bigrams | S18, S36–S38, S50, S51, S67 | Strong | IM77 + Wells; out of sample (S44); earlier in-repo work agrees |
| Slot classes absent in planted syllabic text and Ur III legends | S37, S87, S89, S90 | Strong | Both transcriptions |
| Opener = seal-owning; suffix = tablets/sealings; mutually exclusive | S29, S29b, S66, S86 | Strong | Both transcriptions, within cities |
| Closers are a closed class (~200) that clusters by house and quarter | S40, S54, S62, S63, S63b, S85 | Moderate | Mohenjo-daro specific; Harappa confounded |
| Round (Gulf-type) seal tradition: no home frame; abroad, persons + numbers, "1 + person", own formulae (415-803-1) | S46, S46b, S74, S98b, S99–S104 | Strong (abroad), moderate (at home) | Robustness checks applied |
| Tablets as vouchers: count on one face, text on the other; texts keep fixed counts | S93, S93b, S94 | Moderate–strong | Incised-only control |
| Indus texts are formulae, not sentences (repetition 0.1–0.3 vs 0.7–0.8 for language) | S16, S88, S91b | Strong | Planted syllabic and logographic controls |
| Typology: suffixing, modifier before head (excludes West Asian head-initial languages) | S33, S35, S43 | Moderate (depends on reading direction) | — |

### Details

**No dictionary, and the key search could never have produced one.**
- Sumerian, Akkadian, Tamil and Sanskrit (on middles) all score on Indus inside the range they reach on other-language corpora; Telugu and Malayalam had no power (S8, S8b, S31, S55).
- **More fundamentally, the audit (S107, S113) shows that passing keys recover almost none of the true readings, even in planted controls and in real Ur III seal legends** (0–3 of 80). The window-hit objective prefers degenerate keys: the true planted key scores 1,313 hits vs 3,514 for the found key.
- So every earlier "PASS" or "power OK" detected language-derived text, not a correct key. Forcing a one-to-one key does not help: wrong injective keys still beat the true key, 2,339–2,483 vs 1,313 hits (S114). The objective is non-identifiable at this corpus size. More text does not help (S115). A whole-text coverage objective with a one-to-one key parses 100% of a planted Tamil corpus with 0 correct signs, better than the true key's 67% (S116, S117). **Short formulaic texts plus a large lexicon always admit a perfect-looking wrong reading.** Even with the 40 commonest signs anchored to their true values, the search recovers only 6 of the other 40 (S118), and a 15× larger corpus does not change this (S119). A syllable language model, the classical cipher-breaking objective, is also fooled (S120). So are clean whole-word texts (S122), and so is the exact true vocabulary (S123). The contrast case shows why: with a small syllabary of 80 signs the truth becomes the optimum (S124). With 150 or 300 signs it no longer is when only the top 80 are decoded (S125), it seemed to be again when *all* signs were decoded (S126), but a longer search finds wrong keys that beat the truth there too (S127). **What works:** with about 40 of 150 signs anchored from outside and the right vocabulary, the search recovers 86% of the rest (S129), and 56% even with a generic large dictionary. With 20 anchors it recovers only 20% (S130). A threshold of about 15–25% of the inventory is the target for any future decipherment. For a logo-syllabic script where the common words are word-signs (as the Indus frame suggests), about 20 anchors suffice with a domain vocabulary and about 40 with a generic dictionary (S135, S136). **The barrier is the long tail of rare signs in very short texts, plus an unknown language and vocabulary**, and a large wrong lexicon still fits perfectly (S117).
- Structure also argues against a phonetic key: repetition behaves like formulaic legends (S9, S16, S26, S88); doubled signs change function rather than spell syllables (S57, S58); foreign residues are too short for syllabic names (S53); sign variants do not track context (S22).

**What was found instead: a grammar, from data only** (GRAMMAR.md). Note: the opener / core / pre-ending / ending frame and the two stroke uses were already in `docs/INDUS-DECIPHERMENT-ATTEMPT.md`. This push re-derived them independently (from IM77) and added the controls and the new findings listed in GRAMMAR.md, "Relation to earlier work".

1. **A seal-text frame.** Opener (267-99 / 391-99 / 293-123-343) + unique middle + closer (X + jar/211/15/12/162/169) + optional suffix (176/1) (S18, S24, S36–S38).
   - Found without supervision (S37) and replicated on the independent Wells transcription (S30, S66, S67).
   - Holds out of sample on post-1977 sites (S44).
   - Its non-local constraints go beyond bigrams (S50, S51, S67).
2. **Name-like middles and title-like closers.**
   - Middles are unique and city-local (S17, S76) and combine freely with closers (S32); some are compounds (S34, S35).
   - Closers are a closed class (S40, S54), shared across cities (S25) yet clustered by house and quarter within Mohenjo-daro (S62, S63, S63b).
   - Quantities sit in the closing part (S68); each frame slot has its own human-figure sign (S77, S72).
3. **Two sub-systems in one grammar.** The opener goes with owned seals and the post-jar suffix with sealings, tablets and rods, within each city (S29, S29b, S66). They exclude each other inside a text (S51, S67).
4. **Two numeral usages.** Short and tall strokes pair with disjoint item classes (S70, S70b), but most are fixed number-compounds (S71); genuine counting shows mainly on trees (S23).
5. **The round-seal tradition.** Round (Gulf-type) and cylinder seals lack the home owner frame, even when found in Indus cities (S46; robust at known sites, S46b).
   - Found abroad, they carry person + number texts with the person sign first (S47, S74). Square Indus seals and pots abroad keep the home grammar (S74).
   - Numbered persons occur in 10 of 56 texts of this tradition vs 6 of 3,194 home texts (S98b); at home persons are never counted (S98).
   - It has its own formulae: 415-803-1-… at Ur and Dilmun, "1 + person" beside unique name-like strings (S99–S104). Its "1 + person" matches the Mesopotamian "1(diš) + name" way of listing individuals (S101).
   - Round seals found at home instead carry W140/W592 (S106), so the round format at home and abroad is not one homogeneous group.
   - The tradition favours the short-horned bull emblem (S65, S65b).
   - This is the best-defined population for a future outside anchor (ANCHORS §18).
5b. **Tablets as vouchers.** Two-sided Harappa tablets put a count on one face and a text on the other, almost never two counts (4 vs 34 expected, S94).
   - Texts keep a fixed count above chance, even on hand-incised tablets (S93, S93b).
   - The main multi-value voucher formula sits in one area of Mound F (S96).
   - The standard value may shift from 4 to 3 over time (S95, weakened by S95b).
6. **Typology.** Markers follow their hosts and modifiers precede heads (S33, S35, S36). This excludes head-initial West Asian languages as the frame's language, but it cannot separate Dravidian, Indo-Aryan, Munda or Burushaski (S43).
7. **Time.** The grammar is stable through Mohenjo-daro's sequence (S45). Tablet texts lengthen from Harappa 3A/B to 3C (S49), and the frame erodes at Dholavira stage 6 (S45).

**Controls added late in the push:**
- The paradigm finder finds no slot classes in planted syllabic text or Ur III legends, and only a few in Proto-Elamite (S87, S89, S90).
- Language-like text, syllabic or logographic, repeats signs at 0.7–0.8 of chance vs Indus 0.1–0.3 (S88).
- Long Indus texts are stacked formulae (S91, S91b).

**Corrections made during the push:**
- S9 was too strong; it is transcription-dependent (S16).
- S24's "prefixes" are middle-initial (S38).
- S70's "metrological systems" are mostly fixed compounds (S71).
- S63 at Harappa may be a phase effect (S63b).
- S17's "short texts spread" part did not replicate (S76).
- S46's person-sign enrichment at home was not robust (S46b); nor was S65's emblem at home (S65c).

**Frozen for new data:** R1–R13 in `prereg/preregistration-3-frozen.json`.
